## 2026-05-21 — Sink: strftime in pcap output path; pre-create today + tomorrow dirs

**Authority:** implementation (Claude Code)

**What changed:**
- The tcpdump `-w` argument uses strftime in the directory portion as well as the filename: `${CAPTURE_DIR}/%Y/%m/%d/${SOURCE_TAG}/cap-%Y%m%d-%H%M%S.pcap`. This means each rotation that crosses midnight UTC lands in the new day's directory automatically.
- `capture-start` pre-creates today's AND tomorrow's `YYYY/MM/DD/${SOURCE_TAG}/` directories at launch.
- `capture-watchdog` (running every 60s) refreshes both directories defensively.
- Both directories are created with `install -d -o capture -g capture -m 0750`.

**Why:**
- `docs/sink/README.md` §4 had two contradictory statements: the literal `-w` example used `$(date -u +%Y/%m/%d)` (shell-substituted, fixed at startup), while the narrative said "on rotation, the new file is created in whatever path the strftime expands to" — which only works if strftime is in the path.
- The strftime-in-path form is the better choice: pcaps from any day-of-capture live under that day's directory regardless of when `capture.service` was started or restarted. Cleaner querying, cleaner pruning.
- The midnight UTC race (tcpdump rolls over before the new day's dir exists) is closed by pre-creating tomorrow's dir at startup and every watchdog tick. A continuously-running sink will always have tomorrow's dir in place well before midnight.

**Affected docs:**
- `docs/sink/README.md` §4 — updated example to use `%Y/%m/%d` instead of `$(date)`.

---

## 2026-05-21 — Sink: helper-script env overrides for testability

**Authority:** implementation (Claude Code)

**What changed:**
- `capture-start`, `capture-watchdog`, and `capture-healthcheck` each honor a `CAPTURE_SINK_CONF` env variable that overrides the default config path (`/etc/capture-sink/sink.conf`).
- `capture-healthcheck` additionally honors `HEALTHCHECK_SKIP_SERVICE=1`, which skips the `systemctl is-active capture.service` check.
- These exist to let `sink/test/smoke.sh` exercise the helpers end-to-end on a single host without requiring the production install (no `/etc/capture-sink/`, no enabled systemd units).

**Why:**
- The smoke test runs `tcpdump` directly via `capture-start` against a veth pair, with a throwaway config in `/tmp`. Without the env overrides, it would either need to clobber `/etc/capture-sink/sink.conf` (bad) or skip helper testing (worse — the helpers are the part most likely to have bugs).
- Both overrides default off; production install is unaffected.

**Affected docs:**
- `sink/README.md` (testing section)
- `sink/test/README.md`

---

## 2026-05-21 — Sink: systemd unit hardening profile

**Authority:** implementation (Claude Code)

**What changed:**
- `capture.service` and `capture-watchdog.service` apply a strict sandbox profile: `ProtectSystem=strict`, `ProtectHome=true`, `ProtectKernel*=true`, `ProtectControlGroups=true`, `RestrictAddressFamilies=AF_UNIX AF_NETLINK AF_PACKET`, `NoNewPrivileges=true`, `SystemCallFilter=@system-service` minus `@privileged @resources @debug @mount`, `MemoryDenyWriteExecute=true`, `LockPersonality=true`.
- `ReadWritePaths` is restricted to `/captures` and `/var/lib/capture-sink`.
- The capture service runs as `User=root` initially (needs CAP_NET_RAW + CAP_NET_ADMIN to open AF_PACKET); `tcpdump -Z capture` then drops to the unprivileged `capture` user after the socket is open. `NoNewPrivileges=true` is compatible with this — setresuid() is dropping privileges, not gaining them.
- `StartLimitIntervalSec=120` and `StartLimitBurst=5` placed in `[Unit]` (current systemd) so the sink doesn't crash-loop forever on a config mistake.

**Why:**
- `docs/sink/README.md` §11 ("Things deliberately left to Claude Code's judgment") explicitly invited a sensible hardening default.
- `RestrictAddressFamilies` is the load-bearing one — it limits what a compromised tcpdump can do post-`-Z` (no IPv4/IPv6 sockets, can't exfiltrate over the management interface).
- `ProtectSystem=strict` with explicit `ReadWritePaths` makes `/etc`, `/usr`, and the rest of the rootfs read-only to tcpdump, mitigating disk-write CVEs in libpcap/tcpdump.

**Affected docs:**
- `sink/systemd/capture.service` and `sink/systemd/capture-watchdog.service` carry the directives.

---

## 2026-05-21 — Default capture sink: dedicated vnet termination

**Authority:** design (chat session)

**What changed:**
- The capture sink terminates GRE/ERSPAN tunnels on a NIC attached to a **dedicated** `vnet-transport`, isolated from all scenario vnets.
- Sink has exactly two NICs: `eth0` on `vnet-mgmt` (SSH, metrics), `eth1` on `vnet-transport` (capture, promiscuous, no usable IP for scenario traffic).
- Sink is not reachable from any scenario vnet — only mirror-egress traffic enters via the GRE tunnels.
- Recommended termination: kernel `gretap` interface with `remote 0.0.0.0` to accept tunnels from any zone; `tcpdump` runs on the decapsulated interface, not on the raw underlay.

**Why:**
- Isolation: a compromised scenario host cannot scan, probe, or attack the sink.
- No accidental capture loops (sink can't see its own GRE traffic if not on a scenario vnet).
- Independent capacity sizing for the mirror plane.
- Drop-in mapping to cloud equivalents (AWS VPC Traffic Mirroring target subnets, Azure vTAP) when the cloud adapter is added later.

**Affected docs:**
- `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §3 (Mirror plane), §5 (Capture sink)
- `docs/sink/README.md` — interface contract

---

## 2026-05-21 — Default capture sink: `tcpdump` black box with rotation + disk watchdog

**Authority:** design (chat session)

**What changed:**
- The default capture sink is a Linux VM (or bare-metal box) running `tcpdump` in promiscuous mode on the capture NIC.
- Rotation by both time (`-G 300` = 5 min) and size (`-C 1024` = 1024 MB ≈ 1 GB), whichever fires first. `-W 0` (unlimited file count — retention is the watchdog's job, not tcpdump's).
- Filenames embed full UTC timestamps via strftime (`cap-%Y%m%d-%H%M%S.pcap`).
- Post-rotate hook compresses with `zstd -1` (or `gzip -1`).
- `tcpdump` drops privileges to a `capture` user via `-Z`.
- `--immediate-mode` and a 512 MB kernel ring buffer (`-B 524288`) by default to absorb bursts.
- A separate **disk watchdog** runs as a systemd timer every 60s. Two thresholds: soft (80% — start pruning oldest pcaps until 70%), hard (92% — alert, optionally stop capture). Always keeps the newest N files (default 10) to avoid eating the active write target.
- The whole sink is a **black box**: input is a NIC, output is a directory of pcaps, contract is a config file. Internals are replaceable without affecting the rest of the platform.

**Why:**
- Simplest thing that works for v1 — covers the 90% case.
- Decouples capture from retention: each process does one thing.
- The `-W 0` choice is deliberate — using `tcpdump -W <n>` triggers numeric-counter filename mode and silently overwrites old files instead of preserving strftime patterns. Watchdog-based retention is the only correct approach.
- Black-box contract means we can swap to `netsniff-ng`, `daemonlogger`, or PF_RING later without touching anything upstream.

**Affected docs:**
- `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §5
- `docs/sink/README.md` — full spec

---

## 2026-05-21 — Defer scenario-state correlation in capture pipeline

**Authority:** user

**What changed:**
- The v1 capture pipeline does **not** correlate captured pcaps with scenario/run/host/phase identifiers.
- Pcaps are organized by `(date, tunnel source)` only — e.g. `/captures/2026/05/20/zone-a/cap-114703.pcap.zst`.
- No run manifest, no indexer, no flow database, no in-band tagging.
- The user discriminates runs by timestamp ranges externally.
- This is a **deliberate deferral**, not a "do this never." Revisit when the volume of runs makes manual time-range queries painful.

**Why:**
- Avoids building a full telemetry-correlation system before it's needed.
- The earlier proposed design (six-level ID hierarchy, run manifests, indexer-keyed flows DB) is sound but premature for v1.
- Adding correlation later is non-destructive: pcaps written under the simpler scheme can be re-indexed by a future correlator that reads sidecar metadata.

**Affected docs:**
- `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §5 (Capture sink), §6 (Future work)
- `docs/OPEN_QUESTIONS.md` — listed as "deferred, revisit when needed"

**Supersedes:** (the earlier in-session proposal of run-manifest + indexer — not committed, no prior entry to supersede formally)

---

## 2026-05-21 — Capture path: Proxmox SDN with OVS-backed bridges and per-VM mirrors

**Authority:** design (chat session)

**What changed:**
- For scenarios that need traffic capture, the adapter creates the scenario bridge as an **OVS bridge**, not a Linux bridge (which has no port-mirror support).
- SDN zone type is **Simple or VLAN**, backed by OVS — not VXLAN/EVPN (those encapsulate frames before mirror visibility).
- Each tap point is a **per-VM OVS mirror object** (`ovs-vsctl create mirror name=mir-<vmid> …`) — granular control, named per source, easy to add/remove as a scenario evolves.
- Mirrors are installed via Proxmox **VM hook scripts** (`post-start`) and torn down on `pre-stop`. This is mandatory because the VM's tap interface (`tap<vmid>i0`) is recreated on every boot — a mirror referencing the old tap port goes stale and silently captures nothing.
- For cross-host mirroring (source VM on node A, sink on node B), the mirror's `output-port` is an **OVS GRE tunnel** port, terminating on the sink. This is functionally ERSPAN.
- Default-bridge VLAN tags are preserved by the mirror — Zeek/Suricata downstream can attribute traffic per scenario vnet without extra config.

**Why:**
- The Proxmox default network stack uses Linux bridges, which cannot mirror at all. Using defaults here would silently produce empty captures — the most dangerous failure mode.
- Proxmox SDN today does not expose mirror configuration in its REST API; the adapter has to drop down to `ovs-vsctl` directly via SSH or a node-local agent.
- Hook scripts make mirrors **stateless and self-healing** — they're rebuilt from the deploy plan on every VM start, so they survive VM reboots, node reboots, and snapshot restores without operator intervention.

**Affected docs:**
- `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §3 (Mirror plane)
- `docs/proxmox-adapter/mirroring.md` (to be drafted by Claude Code or in a later chat)

---

## 2026-05-21 — Canvas-to-deploy pipeline: six-stage with platform-agnostic IR

**Authority:** design (chat session)

**What changed:**
- The deployment pipeline has six stages: **Canvas → Scenario IR → Validator → Planner → Adapter → Runtime**, with a Scenario State Store as a side-channel.
- The **Scenario IR** is YAML + JSON schema, platform-agnostic (uses neutral terms `host`, `network`, `switch` — never `qemu vm`, `lxc container`, `ec2 instance`).
- The IR is the single source of truth between the canvas and any backend. Adapters are pluggable per platform.
- The validator runs before any deploy: schema check, cross-layer binding check (every app references an existing host, etc.), capacity check, image-availability check, network-sanity check (CIDR collisions, VLAN ID conflicts).
- The planner produces an **ordered DAG** of operations, not a flat list. Enables partial redeploys.
- v1 target adapter: **Proxmox**. VMware and cloud adapters are out of scope for v1 but the architecture is designed to accept them without changing the canvas, IR, validator, or planner.

**Why:**
- Indirection via IR means swapping backends doesn't require rewriting the canvas or the AI agents.
- DAG-based planning enables partial redeploys (change one app, only that node + downstream activity needs reconciliation).
- Catching errors at validation time (before any VM is created) saves operator hours.

**Affected docs:**
- `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §2 (Canvas-to-deploy pipeline)
- `docs/ir/README.md` — IR schema sketch (in `OPEN_QUESTIONS.md` until ratified)

---

## 2026-05-21 — Three primary layers, with optional overlays

**Authority:** design (chat session)

**What changed:**
- The topology canvas has **three primary layers**: Infrastructure / Apps & Tools / Activity.
- Three **optional overlays** can be enabled per scenario: Identity, Data, Defense.
- Overlays are not always-present layers — they appear in the canvas only for scenarios that need them.

**Why:**
- Three primary layers map cleanly to the three questions a scenario designer asks: *what's wired? · what's running? · what's happening?*
- Beyond three, the designer pays cognitive tax for planes that are empty in most scenarios.
- Identity/Data/Defense matter for serious scenarios (ransomware, detection engineering) but not for simpler ones — making them overlays gives precision where it earns its keep, silence everywhere else.

**Affected docs:**
- `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §1 (Canvas + layers)
